People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I came because I’m spending just far too much of my day checking things in the house. It’s mostly folders, drawers, and these labeled boxes I keep for important papers and little keepsakes—things I really don’t want misplaced. I’ll open a folder to make sure the documents are still there, then close it, and then a few minutes later I feel I have to open it again. Same with drawers and boxes. I know I just looked, I do know that, but it doesn’t seem to settle it for me.

It’s gotten to where I’ll go around checking the same places again and again, and it takes up a ridiculous amount of time. I feel embarrassed saying that at my age, but it’s become hard to just leave things alone once I’ve checked them.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do. It’s not just that I remember to check something, it’s more like a thought starts poking at me and won’t leave me alone. I’ll think, “What if I threw away my husband’s old tax papers?” even though I know perfectly well I wouldn’t just toss something like that without noticing. Or I’ll suddenly worry that I put one of the family recipes in the wrong box, the ones I try to keep separate because they mean something to me.

And the thing is, I can tell myself, “You know better, you’re careful, you already looked,” but it doesn’t seem to matter much. The thought keeps needling at me, like I’ve done something terribly careless and just haven’t discovered it yet. It makes me feel very uneasy, almost guilty, until I go and make sure.

I’ll open a folder to make sure the documents are still there, then close it, and then a few minutes later I feel I have to open it again. Same with drawers and boxes. I know I just looked, I do know that, but it doesn’t seem to settle it for me.\n\nIt’s gotten to where I’ll go around checking the same places again and again, and it takes up a ridiculous amount of time. I feel embarrassed saying that at my age, but it’s become hard to just leave things alone once I’ve checked them.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and make you feel anxious or uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not just that I remember to check something, it’s more like a thought starts poking at me and won’t leave me alone. I’ll think, “What if I threw away my husband’s old tax papers?” even though I know perfectly well I wouldn’t just toss something like that without noticing. Or I’ll suddenly worry that I put one of the family recipes in the wrong box, the ones I try to keep separate because they mean something to me.\n\nAnd the thing is, I can tell myself, “You know better, you’re careful, you already looked,” but it doesn’t seem to matter much. The thought keeps needling at me, like I’ve done something terribly careless and just haven’t discovered it yet. It makes me feel very uneasy, almost guilty, until I go and make sure.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you have to check these things in a specific way or order to feel okay?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not exactly in a neat order, no. It’s more that I feel I have to open the same folder or drawer or one of those labeled boxes and look inside until I’m convinced, at least for the moment, that the papers or keepsakes are still where I left them.

Sometimes I’ll check a folder, close it, walk away, and then almost immediately I’m thinking, “Did I really see it?” So I go back and open it again. And then I might move to a drawer, then back to the same box, especially if it has something important in it. It isn’t like a ritual with counting or anything like that, but it has to be checked properly, if that makes sense—actually looking, not just glancing. Otherwise I don’t feel settled. Even when I only checked a few minutes before, I’ll still feel pulled to do it again.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… that’s a good way to put it, unfortunately. I don’t feel like I’m choosing it in the ordinary sense, like, “Oh, I’ll just tidy up and look through that drawer.” It feels more like I can’t leave it alone once the thought is there.

I’ll have just opened a folder and seen the paper sitting right there, or checked one of my labeled boxes and seen the keepsake where it belongs, and I’ll close it and think, “All right, that’s done.” But then a few minutes later there’s this pressure to go back. It’s as if my mind won’t accept the answer I just gave it. I know I checked, but I still feel pushed to open it again and make sure.

So yes, I do feel forced by it, in a way. Not by anyone else, of course, but by this uncomfortable feeling that I won’t be settled unless I check the same folders, drawers, and boxes again. And it’s taking up far too much of my day.

It’s not just that I remember to check something, it’s more like a thought starts poking at me and won’t leave me alone. I’ll think, “What if I threw away my husband’s old tax papers?” even though I know perfectly well I wouldn’t just toss something like that without noticing. Or I’ll suddenly worry that I put one of the family recipes in the wrong box, the ones I try to keep separate because they mean something to me.\n\nAnd the thing is, I can tell myself, “You know better, you’re careful, you already looked,” but it doesn’t seem to matter much. The thought keeps needling at me, like I’ve done something terribly careless and just haven’t discovered it yet. It makes me feel very uneasy, almost guilty, until I go and make sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check these things in a specific way or order to feel okay?\n\nPatient: Not exactly in a neat order, no. It’s more that I feel I have to open the same folder or drawer or one of those labeled boxes and look inside until I’m convinced, at least for the moment, that the papers or keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nSometimes I’ll check a folder, close it, walk away, and then almost immediately I’m thinking, “Did I really see it?” So I go back and open it again. And then I might move to a drawer, then back to the same box, especially if it has something important in it. It isn’t like a ritual with counting or anything like that, but it has to be checked properly, if that makes sense—actually looking, not just glancing. Otherwise I don’t feel settled. Even when I only checked a few minutes before, I’ll still feel pulled to do it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have no choice but to check these things, like you're being forced to do it?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s a good way to put it, unfortunately. I don’t feel like I’m choosing it in the ordinary sense, like, “Oh, I’ll just tidy up and look through that drawer.” It feels more like I can’t leave it alone once the thought is there.\n\nI’ll have just opened a folder and seen the paper sitting right there, or checked one of my labeled boxes and seen the keepsake where it belongs, and I’ll close it and think, “All right, that’s done.” But then a few minutes later there’s this pressure to go back. It’s as if my mind won’t accept the answer I just gave it. I know I checked, but I still feel pushed to open it again and make sure.\n\nSo yes, I do feel forced by it, in a way. Not by anyone else, of course, but by this uncomfortable feeling that I won’t be settled unless I check the same folders, drawers, and boxes again. And it’s taking up far too much of my day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not washing my hands or tapping, no, not really like that. It’s more the opening and looking that I seem to get stuck doing.

I came because I’m spending far too much of my day going back to the same folders, the same drawers, and those labeled boxes I keep for important papers and keepsakes. I’ll open one to make sure something is where I left it, and even if I see it there, even if I only checked a few minutes before, I feel this need to open it again and look properly. It’s a physical thing in the sense that I’m getting up, pulling the drawer out, lifting the lid, sorting through the folder, making sure the paper or item is actually there.

So no, it isn’t a particular action like tapping three times or washing, but I do feel I have to do that checking action over and over before I can feel okay, at least for a little while.

It isn’t like a ritual with counting or anything like that, but it has to be checked properly, if that makes sense—actually looking, not just glancing. Otherwise I don’t feel settled. Even when I only checked a few minutes before, I’ll still feel pulled to do it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have no choice but to check these things, like you're being forced to do it?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s a good way to put it, unfortunately. I don’t feel like I’m choosing it in the ordinary sense, like, “Oh, I’ll just tidy up and look through that drawer.” It feels more like I can’t leave it alone once the thought is there.\n\nI’ll have just opened a folder and seen the paper sitting right there, or checked one of my labeled boxes and seen the keepsake where it belongs, and I’ll close it and think, “All right, that’s done.” But then a few minutes later there’s this pressure to go back. It’s as if my mind won’t accept the answer I just gave it. I know I checked, but I still feel pushed to open it again and make sure.\n\nSo yes, I do feel forced by it, in a way. Not by anyone else, of course, but by this uncomfortable feeling that I won’t be settled unless I check the same folders, drawers, and boxes again. And it’s taking up far too much of my day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel the need to perform any specific physical actions, like washing your hands or tapping, in order to feel okay?\n\nPatient: Not washing my hands or tapping, no, not really like that. It’s more the opening and looking that I seem to get stuck doing.\n\nI came because I’m spending far too much of my day going back to the same folders, the same drawers, and those labeled boxes I keep for important papers and keepsakes. I’ll open one to make sure something is where I left it, and even if I see it there, even if I only checked a few minutes before, I feel this need to open it again and look properly. It’s a physical thing in the sense that I’m getting up, pulling the drawer out, lifting the lid, sorting through the folder, making sure the paper or item is actually there.\n\nSo no, it isn’t a particular action like tapping three times or washing, but I do feel I have to do that checking action over and over before I can feel okay, at least for a little while.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"intent": "Determine if the patient's repetitive checking behavior is causing significant distress or impairment in social, occupational, or other important areas of functioning", "question": "Is this checking behavior affecting your relationships or daily activities in any significant way?"}

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, it is. More than I like to admit, honestly.

I’m not working now, so I used to think, “Well, at least it’s only my own time I’m wasting,” but it isn’t just that. I’ve had to cancel mentoring sessions because I’ll start checking a folder or one of those boxes before I’m meant to leave or get on the phone, and then suddenly the whole afternoon has slipped away. By the time I look up, I’m upset with myself and it feels too late or I’m too flustered to go through with it properly.

And I’ve skipped book club too, which bothers me because that’s one of the things I really look forward to. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to make sure one thing is put away, and then I get caught going back through the drawers and boxes again, and then I’m late, and then embarrassed, and then I just don’t go.

It affects having people over as well. My apartment gets left in disarray from all the checking—folders out, drawers open, things not put back neatly because I’m still “making sure,” if that makes sense. Then I feel too embarrassed to invite anyone in or have anyone stop by. So yes, it’s interfering with my day and with seeing people. It’s not destroying everything, but it’s significant enough that I feel quite ashamed of how much it’s narrowing my life.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came because I’m spending just far too much of my day checking things in the house. It’s mostly folders, drawers, and these labeled boxes I keep for important papers and little keepsakes—things I really don’t want misplaced. I’ll open a folder to make sure the documents are still there, then close it, and then a few minutes later I feel I have to open it again. Same with drawers and boxes. I know I just looked, I do know that, but it doesn’t seem to settle it for me.\n\nIt’s gotten to where I’ll go around checking the same places again and again, and it takes up a ridiculous amount of time. By the time I look up, I’m upset with myself and it feels too late or I’m too flustered to go through with it properly.\n\nAnd I’ve skipped book club too, which bothers me because that’s one of the things I really look forward to. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to make sure one thing is put away, and then I get caught going back through the drawers and boxes again, and then I’m late, and then embarrassed, and then I just don’t go.\n\nIt affects having people over as well. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… though it isn’t usually that I think something dramatic will happen, like a fire or an accident or anything like that. It’s more that I get this awful thought that I’ve been terribly careless with something important.

For example, even when part of me knows better, I’ll think, “What if I threw away my husband’s old tax papers?” or, “What if I put that family recipe in the wrong box and now I won’t be able to find it when someone asks for it?” And I can tell myself, “No, you just saw it,” or “You wouldn’t have thrown that out,” but the thought keeps needling at me. It doesn’t feel satisfied with a reasonable answer.

So I suppose the bad thing I’m afraid of is that I’ll have lost or misplaced something that mattered, and that it would be my fault because I didn’t check carefully enough. It feels like I’ve done something terribly irresponsible, even when I know, rationally, that I probably haven’t. And that feeling is what sends me back to the box or the folder again.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came because I’m spending just far too much of my day checking things in the house. It’s mostly folders, drawers, and these labeled boxes I keep for important papers and little keepsakes—things I really don’t want misplaced. I’ll open a folder to make sure the documents are still there, then close it, and then a few minutes later I feel I have to open it again. Same with drawers and boxes. I know I just looked, I do know that, but it doesn’t seem to settle it for me.\n\nIt’s gotten to where I’ll go around checking the same places again and again, and it takes up a ridiculous amount of time. I feel embarrassed saying that at my age, but it’s become hard to just leave things alone once I’ve checked them.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and make you feel anxious or uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not just that I remember to check something, it’s more like a thought starts poking at me and won’t leave me alone. I’ll think, “What if I threw away my husband’s old tax papers?” even though I know perfectly well I wouldn’t just toss something like that without noticing. Or I’ll suddenly worry that I put one of the family recipes in the wrong box, the ones I try to keep separate because they mean something to me.\n\nAnd the thing is, I can tell myself, “You know better, you’re careful, you already looked,” but it doesn’t seem to matter much. The thought keeps needling at me, like I’ve done something terribly careless and just haven’t discovered it yet. It makes me feel very uneasy, almost guilty, until I go and make sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check these things in a specific way or order to feel okay?\n\nPatient: Not exactly in a neat order, no. It’s more that I feel I have to open the same folder or drawer or one of those labeled boxes and look inside until I’m convinced, at least for the moment, that the papers or keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nSometimes I’ll check a folder, close it, walk away, and then almost immediately I’m thinking, “Did I really see it?” So I go back and open it again. And then I might move to a drawer, then back to the same box, especially if it has something important in it. It isn’t like a ritual with counting or anything like that, but it has to be checked properly, if that makes sense—actually looking, not just glancing. Otherwise I don’t feel settled. Even when I only checked a few minutes before, I’ll still feel pulled to do it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have no choice but to check these things, like you're being forced to do it?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s a good way to put it, unfortunately. I don’t feel like I’m choosing it in the ordinary sense, like, “Oh, I’ll just tidy up and look through that drawer.” It feels more like I can’t leave it alone once the thought is there.\n\nI’ll have just opened a folder and seen the paper sitting right there, or checked one of my labeled boxes and seen the keepsake where it belongs, and I’ll close it and think, “All right, that’s done.” But then a few minutes later there’s this pressure to go back. It doesn’t feel satisfied with a reasonable answer.\n\nSo I suppose the bad thing I’m afraid of is that I’ll have lost or misplaced something that mattered, and that it would be my fault because I didn’t check carefully enough. It feels like I’ve done something terribly irresponsible, even when I know, rationally, that I probably haven’t. And that feeling is what sends me back to the box or the folder again.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s mostly that, yes—the thought that something important has been lost or put in the wrong place. But it has a kind of… moral feeling to it, if that makes sense. It isn’t just, “Oh dear, where is that paper?” It becomes, “How could I have been so careless?” or “What if I threw away my husband’s old tax papers and now they’re gone because of me?” Even when I know, really, that I’m careful with those things.

Or with something like a family recipe—I’ll think, “What if I put it in the wrong box?” and then my mind won’t let it rest there. It starts feeling as though I’ve failed in some important responsibility, even if I have no real evidence that anything is missing. I can picture the folder, I can remember seeing it, and still this little needling thought keeps going: maybe you didn’t look properly, maybe it wasn’t the right paper, maybe you only assumed it was there.

So no, I don’t think it’s a lot of different fears, exactly. It’s that fear of having misplaced or thrown away something meaningful, and underneath that, the awful feeling that I’ve done something terribly careless and can’t just let it pass.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came because I’m spending just far too much of my day checking things in the house. It’s mostly folders, drawers, and these labeled boxes I keep for important papers and little keepsakes—things I really don’t want misplaced. I’ll open a folder to make sure the documents are still there, then close it, and then a few minutes later I feel I have to open it again. Same with drawers and boxes. I know I just looked, I do know that, but it doesn’t seem to settle it for me.\n\nIt’s gotten to where I’ll go around checking the same places again and again, and it takes up a ridiculous amount of time. I feel embarrassed saying that at my age, but it’s become hard to just leave things alone once I’ve checked them.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and make you feel anxious or uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not just that I remember to check something, it’s more like a thought starts poking at me and won’t leave me alone. I’ll think, “What if I threw away my husband’s old tax papers?” even though I know perfectly well I wouldn’t just toss something like that without noticing. Or I’ll suddenly worry that I put one of the family recipes in the wrong box, the ones I try to keep separate because they mean something to me.\n\nAnd the thing is, I can tell myself, “You know better, you’re careful, you already looked,” but it doesn’t seem to matter much. The thought keeps needling at me, like I’ve done something terribly careless and just haven’t discovered it yet. It makes me feel very uneasy, almost guilty, until I go and make sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check these things in a specific way or order to feel okay?\n\nPatient: Not exactly in a neat order, no. It’s more that I feel I have to open the same folder or drawer or one of those labeled boxes and look inside until I’m convinced, at least for the moment, that the papers or keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nSometimes I’ll check a folder, close it, walk away, and then almost immediately I’m thinking, “Did I really see it?” So I go back and open it again. And then I might move to a drawer, then back to the same box, especially if it has something important in it. It isn’t like a ritual with counting or anything like that, but it has to be checked properly, if that makes sense—actually looking, not just glancing. Otherwise I don’t feel settled. Even when I only checked a few minutes before, I’ll still feel pulled to do it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have no choice but to check these things, like you're being forced to do it?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s a good way to put it, unfortunately. I don’t feel like I’m choosing it in the ordinary sense, like, “Oh, I’ll just tidy up and look through that drawer.” It feels more like I can’t leave it alone once the thought is there.\n\nI’ll have just opened a folder and seen the paper sitting right there, or checked one of my labeled boxes and seen the keepsake where it belongs, and I’ll close it and think, “All right, that’s done.” But then a few minutes later there’s this pressure to go back. It’s as if my mind won’t accept the answer I just gave it. I know I checked, but I still feel pushed to open it again and make sure.\n\nSo yes, I do feel forced by it, in a way. Not by anyone else, of course, but by this uncomfortable feeling that I won’t be settled unless I check the same folders, drawers, and boxes again. And it’s taking up far too much of my day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel the need to perform any specific physical actions, like washing your hands or tapping, in order to feel okay?\n\nPatient: Not washing my hands or tapping, no, not really like that. It’s more the opening and looking that I seem to get stuck doing.\n\nI came because I’m spending far too much of my day going back to the same folders, the same drawers, and those labeled boxes I keep for important papers and keepsakes. More than I like to admit, honestly.\n\nI’m not working now, so I used to think, “Well, at least it’s only my own time I’m wasting,” but it isn’t just that. I’ve had to cancel mentoring sessions because I’ll start checking a folder or one of those boxes before I’m meant to leave or get on the phone, and then suddenly the whole afternoon has slipped away. By the time I look up, I’m upset with myself and it feels too late or I’m too flustered to go through with it properly.\n\nAnd I’ve skipped book club too, which bothers me because that’s one of the things I really look forward to. It’s not destroying everything, but it’s significant enough that I feel quite ashamed of how much it’s narrowing my life.\n\nDoctor: Are you checking these things because you're afraid something bad will happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: Yes… though it isn’t usually that I think something dramatic will happen, like a fire or an accident or anything like that. I’ll open one to make sure something is where I left it, and even if I see it there, even if I only checked a few minutes before, I feel this need to open it again and look properly. It’s a physical thing in the sense that I’m getting up, pulling the drawer out, lifting the lid, sorting through the folder, making sure the paper or item is actually there.\n\nSo no, it isn’t a particular action like tapping three times or washing, but I do feel I have to do that checking action over and over before I can feel okay, at least for a little while.\n\nDoctor: Is this checking behavior affecting your relationships or daily activities in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. More than I like to admit, honestly.\n\nI’m not working now, so I used to think, “Well, at least it’s only my own time I’m wasting,” but it isn’t just that. I’ve had to cancel mentoring sessions because I’ll start checking a folder or one of those boxes before I’m meant to leave or get on the phone, and then suddenly the whole afternoon has slipped away. By the time I look up, I’m upset with myself and it feels too late or I’m too flustered to go through with it properly.\n\nAnd I’ve skipped book club too, which bothers me because that’s one of the things I really look forward to. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to make sure one thing is put away, and then I get caught going back through the drawers and boxes again, and then I’m late, and then embarrassed, and then I just don’t go.\n\nIt affects having people over as well. My apartment gets left in disarray from all the checking—folders out, drawers open, things not put back neatly because I’m still “making sure,” if that makes sense. Then I feel too embarrassed to invite anyone in or have anyone stop by. So yes, it’s interfering with my day and with seeing people. It’s not destroying everything, but it’s significant enough that I feel quite ashamed of how much it’s narrowing my life.\n\nDoctor: Are you checking these things because you're afraid something bad will happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: Yes… though it isn’t usually that I think something dramatic will happen, like a fire or an accident or anything like that. It’s more that I get this awful thought that I’ve been terribly careless with something important.\n\nFor example, even when part of me knows better, I’ll think, “What if I threw away my husband’s old tax papers?” or, “What if I put that family recipe in the wrong box and now I won’t be able to find it when someone asks for it?” And I can tell myself, “No, you just saw it,” or “You wouldn’t have thrown that out,” but the thought keeps needling at me. It doesn’t feel satisfied with a reasonable answer.\n\nSo I suppose the bad thing I’m afraid of is that I’ll have lost or misplaced something that mattered, and that it would be my fault because I didn’t check carefully enough. It feels like I’ve done something terribly irresponsible, even when I know, rationally, that I probably haven’t. And that feeling is what sends me back to the box or the folder again.\n\nDoctor: Is the thought of something being lost or misplaced the only thing that drives you to check, or are there other fears or concerns behind it?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly that, yes—the thought that something important has been lost or put in the wrong place. But it has a kind of… moral feeling to it, if that makes sense. It isn’t just, “Oh dear, where is that paper?” It becomes, “How could I have been so careless?” or “What if I threw away my husband’s old tax papers and now they’re gone because of me?” Even when I know, really, that I’m careful with those things.\n\nOr with something like a family recipe—I’ll think, “What if I put it in the wrong box?” and then my mind won’t let it rest there. It starts feeling as though I’ve failed in some important responsibility, even if I have no real evidence that anything is missing. I can picture the folder, I can remember seeing it, and still this little needling thought keeps going: maybe you didn’t look properly, maybe it wasn’t the right paper, maybe you only assumed it was there.\n\nSo no, I don’t think it’s a lot of different fears, exactly. It’s that fear of having misplaced or thrown away something meaningful, and underneath that, the awful feeling that I’ve done something terribly careless and can’t just let it pass.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d put it that way, no. I mean, I do like things to be reasonably tidy, but I don’t think I have a particular order they must be in, like everything lined up just so, or arranged by size or color or anything like that.

For me it’s less about how things look and more about whether I can feel sure something is where it’s supposed to be. I might leave things looking quite messy, actually, because I’m still going through them or checking them. So it’s not that I feel comfortable only if the room is perfectly arranged. It’s more… I don’t know, I feel unsettled if I can’t trust that an important paper or keepsake is in the right folder or box.

But no, I haven’t really noticed a need for a specific pattern or arrangement for its own sake. It’s not like the chair has to be angled a certain way or the books have to be in a certain sequence. At least, I don’t think so.

From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came because I’m spending just far too much of my day checking things in the house. It’s mostly folders, drawers, and these labeled boxes I keep for important papers and little keepsakes—things I really don’t want misplaced. I’ll open a folder to make sure the documents are still there, then close it, and then a few minutes later I feel I have to open it again. Same with drawers and boxes. I know I just looked, I do know that, but it doesn’t seem to settle it for me.\n\nIt’s gotten to where I’ll go around checking the same places again and again, and it takes up a ridiculous amount of time. I feel embarrassed saying that at my age, but it’s become hard to just leave things alone once I’ve checked them.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and make you feel anxious or uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not just that I remember to check something, it’s more like a thought starts poking at me and won’t leave me alone. I’ll think, “What if I threw away my husband’s old tax papers?” even though I know perfectly well I wouldn’t just toss something like that without noticing. Or I’ll suddenly worry that I put one of the family recipes in the wrong box, the ones I try to keep separate because they mean something to me.\n\nAnd the thing is, I can tell myself, “You know better, you’re careful, you already looked,” but it doesn’t seem to matter much. The thought keeps needling at me, like I’ve done something terribly careless and just haven’t discovered it yet. It makes me feel very uneasy, almost guilty, until I go and make sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check these things in a specific way or order to feel okay?\n\nPatient: Not exactly in a neat order, no. It’s more that I feel I have to open the same folder or drawer or one of those labeled boxes and look inside until I’m convinced, at least for the moment, that the papers or keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nSometimes I’ll check a folder, close it, walk away, and then almost immediately I’m thinking, “Did I really see it?” So I go back and open it again. And then I might move to a drawer, then back to the same box, especially if it has something important in it. It isn’t like a ritual with counting or anything like that, but it has to be checked properly, if that makes sense—actually looking, not just glancing. Otherwise I don’t feel settled. Even when I only checked a few minutes before, I’ll still feel pulled to do it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have no choice but to check these things, like you're being forced to do it?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s a good way to put it, unfortunately. I don’t feel like I’m choosing it in the ordinary sense, like, “Oh, I’ll just tidy up and look through that drawer.” It feels more like I can’t leave it alone once the thought is there.\n\nI’ll have just opened a folder and seen the paper sitting right there, or checked one of my labeled boxes and seen the keepsake where it belongs, and I’ll close it and think, “All right, that’s done.” But then a few minutes later there’s this pressure to go back. It’s as if my mind won’t accept the answer I just gave it. I know I checked, but I still feel pushed to open it again and make sure.\n\nSo yes, I do feel forced by it, in a way. Not by anyone else, of course, but by this uncomfortable feeling that I won’t be settled unless I check the same folders, drawers, and boxes again. And it’s taking up far too much of my day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel the need to perform any specific physical actions, like washing your hands or tapping, in order to feel okay?\n\nPatient: Not washing my hands or tapping, no, not really like that. It’s more the opening and looking that I seem to get stuck doing.\n\nI came because I’m spending far too much of my day going back to the same folders, the same drawers, and those labeled boxes I keep for important papers and keepsakes. I’ll open one to make sure something is where I left it, and even if I see it there, even if I only checked a few minutes before, I feel this need to open it again and look properly. It’s a physical thing in the sense that I’m getting up, pulling the drawer out, lifting the lid, sorting through the folder, making sure the paper or item is actually there.\n\nSo no, it isn’t a particular action like tapping three times or washing, but I do feel I have to do that checking action over and over before I can feel okay, at least for a little while.\n\nDoctor: Is this checking behavior affecting your relationships or daily activities in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. More than I like to admit, honestly.\n\nI’m not working now, so I used to think, “Well, at least it’s only my own time I’m wasting,” but it isn’t just that. I’ve had to cancel mentoring sessions because I’ll start checking a folder or one of those boxes before I’m meant to leave or get on the phone, and then suddenly the whole afternoon has slipped away. By the time I look up, I’m upset with myself and it feels too late or I’m too flustered to go through with it properly.\n\nAnd I’ve skipped book club too, which bothers me because that’s one of the things I really look forward to. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to make sure one thing is put away, and then I get caught going back through the drawers and boxes again, and then I’m late, and then embarrassed, and then I just don’t go.\n\nIt affects having people over as well. My apartment gets left in disarray from all the checking—folders out, drawers open, things not put back neatly because I’m still “making sure,” if that makes sense. Then I feel too embarrassed to invite anyone in or have anyone stop by. So yes, it’s interfering with my day and with seeing people. It’s not destroying everything, but it’s significant enough that I feel quite ashamed of how much it’s narrowing my life.\n\nDoctor: Are you checking these things because you're afraid something bad will happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: Yes… though it isn’t usually that I think something dramatic will happen, like a fire or an accident or anything like that. It’s more that I get this awful thought that I’ve been terribly careless with something important.\n\nFor example, even when part of me knows better, I’ll think, “What if I threw away my husband’s old tax papers?” or, “What if I put that family recipe in the wrong box and now I won’t be able to find it when someone asks for it?” And I can tell myself, “No, you just saw it,” or “You wouldn’t have thrown that out,” but the thought keeps needling at me. It doesn’t feel satisfied with a reasonable answer.\n\nSo I suppose the bad thing I’m afraid of is that I’ll have lost or misplaced something that mattered, and that it would be my fault because I didn’t check carefully enough. It feels like I’ve done something terribly irresponsible, even when I know, rationally, that I probably haven’t. And that feeling is what sends me back to the box or the folder again.\n\nDoctor: Is the thought of something being lost or misplaced the only thing that drives you to check, or are there other fears or concerns behind it?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly that, yes—the thought that something important has been lost or put in the wrong place. But it has a kind of… moral feeling to it, if that makes sense. It isn’t just, “Oh dear, where is that paper?” It becomes, “How could I have been so careless?” or “What if I threw away my husband’s old tax papers and now they’re gone because of me?” Even when I know, really, that I’m careful with those things.\n\nOr with something like a family recipe—I’ll think, “What if I put it in the wrong box?” and then my mind won’t let it rest there. It starts feeling as though I’ve failed in some important responsibility, even if I have no real evidence that anything is missing. I can picture the folder, I can remember seeing it, and still this little needling thought keeps going: maybe you didn’t look properly, maybe it wasn’t the right paper, maybe you only assumed it was there.\n\nSo no, I don’t think it’s a lot of different fears, exactly. It’s that fear of having misplaced or thrown away something meaningful, and underneath that, the awful feeling that I’ve done something terribly careless and can’t just let it pass.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be in a specific order or arranged in a certain way for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, no. I mean, I do like things to be reasonably tidy, but I don’t think I have a particular order they must be in, like everything lined up just so, or arranged by size or color or anything like that.\n\nFor me it’s less about how things look and more about whether I can feel sure something is where it’s supposed to be. I might leave things looking quite messy, actually, because I’m still going through them or checking them. So it’s not that I feel comfortable only if the room is perfectly arranged. It’s more… I don’t know, I feel unsettled if I can’t trust that an important paper or keepsake is in the right folder or box.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really noticed a need for a specific pattern or arrangement for its own sake. It’s not like the chair has to be angled a certain way or the books have to be in a certain sequence. At least, I don’t think so.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think that’s exactly it. It’s the fear that I’ve made some mistake with something that matters, even if I don’t have a real reason to think I have.

It’s not just, “Where did I put that?” It’s more like this little thought gets stuck in my mind—what if I threw away my husband’s old tax papers, or what if I put that family recipe in the wrong box—and even when I know better, even when I can tell myself I’m careful and I probably just saw it, it keeps needling at me. I can’t seem to just let it pass.

And then it starts to feel as if I’ve done something terribly careless, almost irresponsible, and that’s the part that bothers me so much. I feel I have to check again just to quiet that feeling, even though another part of me knows it doesn’t really make sense.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came because I’m spending just far too much of my day checking things in the house. It’s mostly folders, drawers, and these labeled boxes I keep for important papers and little keepsakes—things I really don’t want misplaced. I’ll open a folder to make sure the documents are still there, then close it, and then a few minutes later I feel I have to open it again. Same with drawers and boxes. I know I just looked, I do know that, but it doesn’t seem to settle it for me.\n\nIt’s gotten to where I’ll go around checking the same places again and again, and it takes up a ridiculous amount of time. I’ll think, “What if I threw away my husband’s old tax papers?” even though I know perfectly well I wouldn’t just toss something like that without noticing. It makes me feel very uneasy, almost guilty, until I go and make sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check these things in a specific way or order to feel okay?\n\nPatient: Not exactly in a neat order, no. It’s more that I feel I have to open the same folder or drawer or one of those labeled boxes and look inside until I’m convinced, at least for the moment, that the papers or keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nSometimes I’ll check a folder, close it, walk away, and then almost immediately I’m thinking, “Did I really see it?” So I go back and open it again. And then I might move to a drawer, then back to the same box, especially if it has something important in it. It isn’t like a ritual with counting or anything like that, but it has to be checked properly, if that makes sense—actually looking, not just glancing. Otherwise I don’t feel settled. Even when I only checked a few minutes before, I’ll still feel pulled to do it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have no choice but to check these things, like you're being forced to do it?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s a good way to put it, unfortunately. I don’t feel like I’m choosing it in the ordinary sense, like, “Oh, I’ll just tidy up and look through that drawer.” It feels more like I can’t leave it alone once the thought is there.\n\nI’ll have just opened a folder and seen the paper sitting right there, or checked one of my labeled boxes and seen the keepsake where it belongs, and I’ll close it and think, “All right, that’s done.” But then a few minutes later there’s this pressure to go back. It’s as if my mind won’t accept the answer I just gave it. I know I checked, but I still feel pushed to open it again and make sure.\n\nSo yes, I do feel forced by it, in a way. Not by anyone else, of course, but by this uncomfortable feeling that I won’t be settled unless I check the same folders, drawers, and boxes again. And it’s taking up far too much of my day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel the need to perform any specific physical actions, like washing your hands or tapping, in order to feel okay?\n\nPatient: Not washing my hands or tapping, no, not really like that. It’s more the opening and looking that I seem to get stuck doing.\n\nI came because I’m spending far too much of my day going back to the same folders, the same drawers, and those labeled boxes I keep for important papers and keepsakes. I’ll open one to make sure something is where I left it, and even if I see it there, even if I only checked a few minutes before, I feel this need to open it again and look properly. It’s a physical thing in the sense that I’m getting up, pulling the drawer out, lifting the lid, sorting through the folder, making sure the paper or item is actually there.\n\nSo no, it isn’t a particular action like tapping three times or washing, but I do feel I have to do that checking action over and over before I can feel okay, at least for a little while.\n\nDoctor: Is this checking behavior affecting your relationships or daily activities in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. More than I like to admit, honestly.\n\nI’m not working now, so I used to think, “Well, at least it’s only my own time I’m wasting,” but it isn’t just that. I’ve had to cancel mentoring sessions because I’ll start checking a folder or one of those boxes before I’m meant to leave or get on the phone, and then suddenly the whole afternoon has slipped away. By the time I look up, I’m upset with myself and it feels too late or I’m too flustered to go through with it properly.\n\nAnd I’ve skipped book club too, which bothers me because that’s one of the things I really look forward to. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to make sure one thing is put away, and then I get caught going back through the drawers and boxes again, and then I’m late, and then embarrassed, and then I just don’t go.\n\nIt affects having people over as well. My apartment gets left in disarray from all the checking—folders out, drawers open, things not put back neatly because I’m still “making sure,” if that makes sense. Then I feel too embarrassed to invite anyone in or have anyone stop by. So yes, it’s interfering with my day and with seeing people. It’s not destroying everything, but it’s significant enough that I feel quite ashamed of how much it’s narrowing my life.\n\nDoctor: Are you checking these things because you're afraid something bad will happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: Yes… though it isn’t usually that I think something dramatic will happen, like a fire or an accident or anything like that. It’s more that I get this awful thought that I’ve been terribly careless with something important.\n\nFor example, even when part of me knows better, I’ll think, “What if I threw away my husband’s old tax papers?” or, “What if I put that family recipe in the wrong box and now I won’t be able to find it when someone asks for it?” And I can tell myself, “No, you just saw it,” or “You wouldn’t have thrown that out,” but the thought keeps needling at me. It doesn’t feel satisfied with a reasonable answer.\n\nSo I suppose the bad thing I’m afraid of is that I’ll have lost or misplaced something that mattered, and that it would be my fault because I didn’t check carefully enough. It feels like I’ve done something terribly irresponsible, even when I know, rationally, that I probably haven’t. And that feeling is what sends me back to the box or the folder again.\n\nDoctor: Is the thought of something being lost or misplaced the only thing that drives you to check, or are there other fears or concerns behind it?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly that, yes—the thought that something important has been lost or put in the wrong place. But it has a kind of… moral feeling to it, if that makes sense. It isn’t just, “Oh dear, where is that paper?” It becomes, “How could I have been so careless?” or “What if I threw away my husband’s old tax papers and now they’re gone because of me?” Even when I know, really, that I’m careful with those things.\n\nOr with something like a family recipe—I’ll think, “What if I put it in the wrong box?” and then my mind won’t let it rest there. It starts feeling as though I’ve failed in some important responsibility, even if I have no real evidence that anything is missing. I can picture the folder, I can remember seeing it, and still this little needling thought keeps going: maybe you didn’t look properly, maybe it wasn’t the right paper, maybe you only assumed it was there.\n\nSo no, I don’t think it’s a lot of different fears, exactly. It’s that fear of having misplaced or thrown away something meaningful, and underneath that, the awful feeling that I’ve done something terribly careless and can’t just let it pass.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be in a specific order or arranged in a certain way for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, no. I mean, I do like things to be reasonably tidy, but I don’t think I have a particular order they must be in, like everything lined up just so, or arranged by size or color or anything like that.\n\nFor me it’s less about how things look and more about whether I can feel sure something is where it’s supposed to be. I might leave things looking quite messy, actually, because I’m still going through them or checking them. So it’s not that I feel comfortable only if the room is perfectly arranged. It’s more… I don’t know, I feel unsettled if I can’t trust that an important paper or keepsake is in the right folder or box.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really noticed a need for a specific pattern or arrangement for its own sake. It’s not like the chair has to be angled a certain way or the books have to be in a certain sequence. At least, I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check things mostly driven by the fear of making a mistake with something important?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s exactly it. It’s the fear that I’ve made some mistake with something that matters, even if I don’t have a real reason to think I have.\n\nIt’s not just, “Where did I put that?” It’s more like this little thought gets stuck in my mind—what if I threw away my husband’s old tax papers, or what if I put that family recipe in the wrong box—and even when I know better, even when I can tell myself I’m careful and I probably just saw it, it keeps needling at me. I can’t seem to just let it pass.\n\nAnd then it starts to feel as if I’ve done something terribly careless, almost irresponsible, and that’s the part that bothers me so much. I feel I have to check again just to quiet that feeling, even though another part of me knows it doesn’t really make sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do. Very much so, I think. It feels like it’s my responsibility to keep track of those things, especially the papers or keepsakes that have some meaning or importance attached to them.

Even when I know, in a sensible part of my mind, that I probably haven’t done anything wrong, the thought will keep needling at me. I’ll think, what if I threw away my husband’s old tax papers? Or what if I put that family recipe in the wrong box, and later no one can find it because of me? And then it doesn’t feel like just a small mistake. It starts to feel as if I’ve been terribly careless, almost as though I’ve failed at something I should have protected.

That’s the part I have trouble letting go of. I can tell myself, “You’re careful, you wouldn’t just toss something important,” but the feeling doesn’t settle until I check. It’s as if I can’t quite allow myself to move on while there’s even the tiniest possibility that I misplaced something important.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came because I’m spending just far too much of my day checking things in the house. It’s mostly folders, drawers, and these labeled boxes I keep for important papers and little keepsakes—things I really don’t want misplaced. I’ll open a folder to make sure the documents are still there, then close it, and then a few minutes later I feel I have to open it again. Same with drawers and boxes. I know I just looked, I do know that, but it doesn’t seem to settle it for me.\n\nIt’s gotten to where I’ll go around checking the same places again and again, and it takes up a ridiculous amount of time. I feel embarrassed saying that at my age, but it’s become hard to just leave things alone once I’ve checked them.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and make you feel anxious or uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not just that I remember to check something, it’s more like a thought starts poking at me and won’t leave me alone. I’ll think, “What if I threw away my husband’s old tax papers?” even though I know perfectly well I wouldn’t just toss something like that without noticing. Or I’ll suddenly worry that I put one of the family recipes in the wrong box, the ones I try to keep separate because they mean something to me.\n\nAnd the thing is, I can tell myself, “You know better, you’re careful, you already looked,” but it doesn’t seem to matter much. The thought keeps needling at me, like I’ve done something terribly careless and just haven’t discovered it yet. It makes me feel very uneasy, almost guilty, until I go and make sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check these things in a specific way or order to feel okay?\n\nPatient: Not exactly in a neat order, no. It’s more that I feel I have to open the same folder or drawer or one of those labeled boxes and look inside until I’m convinced, at least for the moment, that the papers or keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nSometimes I’ll check a folder, close it, walk away, and then almost immediately I’m thinking, “Did I really see it?” So I go back and open it again. And then I might move to a drawer, then back to the same box, especially if it has something important in it. It isn’t like a ritual with counting or anything like that, but it has to be checked properly, if that makes sense—actually looking, not just glancing. Otherwise I don’t feel settled. Even when I only checked a few minutes before, I’ll still feel pulled to do it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have no choice but to check these things, like you're being forced to do it?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s a good way to put it, unfortunately. I don’t feel like I’m choosing it in the ordinary sense, like, “Oh, I’ll just tidy up and look through that drawer.” It feels more like I can’t leave it alone once the thought is there.\n\nI’ll have just opened a folder and seen the paper sitting right there, or checked one of my labeled boxes and seen the keepsake where it belongs, and I’ll close it and think, “All right, that’s done.” But then a few minutes later there’s this pressure to go back. It’s as if my mind won’t accept the answer I just gave it. I know I checked, but I still feel pushed to open it again and make sure.\n\nSo yes, I do feel forced by it, in a way. Not by anyone else, of course, but by this uncomfortable feeling that I won’t be settled unless I check the same folders, drawers, and boxes again. And it’s taking up far too much of my day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel the need to perform any specific physical actions, like washing your hands or tapping, in order to feel okay?\n\nPatient: Not washing my hands or tapping, no, not really like that. It’s more the opening and looking that I seem to get stuck doing.\n\nI came because I’m spending far too much of my day going back to the same folders, the same drawers, and those labeled boxes I keep for important papers and keepsakes. I’ll open one to make sure something is where I left it, and even if I see it there, even if I only checked a few minutes before, I feel this need to open it again and look properly. It’s a physical thing in the sense that I’m getting up, pulling the drawer out, lifting the lid, sorting through the folder, making sure the paper or item is actually there.\n\nSo no, it isn’t a particular action like tapping three times or washing, but I do feel I have to do that checking action over and over before I can feel okay, at least for a little while.\n\nDoctor: Is this checking behavior affecting your relationships or daily activities in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. More than I like to admit, honestly.\n\nI’m not working now, so I used to think, “Well, at least it’s only my own time I’m wasting,” but it isn’t just that. I’ve had to cancel mentoring sessions because I’ll start checking a folder or one of those boxes before I’m meant to leave or get on the phone, and then suddenly the whole afternoon has slipped away. By the time I look up, I’m upset with myself and it feels too late or I’m too flustered to go through with it properly.\n\nAnd I’ve skipped book club too, which bothers me because that’s one of the things I really look forward to. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to make sure one thing is put away, and then I get caught going back through the drawers and boxes again, and then I’m late, and then embarrassed, and then I just don’t go.\n\nIt affects having people over as well. My apartment gets left in disarray from all the checking—folders out, drawers open, things not put back neatly because I’m still “making sure,” if that makes sense. Then I feel too embarrassed to invite anyone in or have anyone stop by. So yes, it’s interfering with my day and with seeing people. It’s not destroying everything, but it’s significant enough that I feel quite ashamed of how much it’s narrowing my life.\n\nDoctor: Are you checking these things because you're afraid something bad will happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: Yes… though it isn’t usually that I think something dramatic will happen, like a fire or an accident or anything like that. It’s more that I get this awful thought that I’ve been terribly careless with something important.\n\nFor example, even when part of me knows better, I’ll think, “What if I threw away my husband’s old tax papers?” or, “What if I put that family recipe in the wrong box and now I won’t be able to find it when someone asks for it?” And I can tell myself, “No, you just saw it,” or “You wouldn’t have thrown that out,” but the thought keeps needling at me. It doesn’t feel satisfied with a reasonable answer.\n\nSo I suppose the bad thing I’m afraid of is that I’ll have lost or misplaced something that mattered, and that it would be my fault because I didn’t check carefully enough. It feels like I’ve done something terribly irresponsible, even when I know, rationally, that I probably haven’t. And that feeling is what sends me back to the box or the folder again.\n\nDoctor: Is the thought of something being lost or misplaced the only thing that drives you to check, or are there other fears or concerns behind it?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly that, yes—the thought that something important has been lost or put in the wrong place. But it has a kind of… moral feeling to it, if that makes sense. It isn’t just, “Oh dear, where is that paper?” It becomes, “How could I have been so careless?” or “What if I threw away my husband’s old tax papers and now they’re gone because of me?” Even when I know, really, that I’m careful with those things.\n\nOr with something like a family recipe—I’ll think, “What if I put it in the wrong box?” and then my mind won’t let it rest there. It starts feeling as though I’ve failed in some important responsibility, even if I have no real evidence that anything is missing. I can picture the folder, I can remember seeing it, and still this little needling thought keeps going: maybe you didn’t look properly, maybe it wasn’t the right paper, maybe you only assumed it was there.\n\nSo no, I don’t think it’s a lot of different fears, exactly. It’s that fear of having misplaced or thrown away something meaningful, and underneath that, the awful feeling that I’ve done something terribly careless and can’t just let it pass.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be in a specific order or arranged in a certain way for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, no. It starts to feel as if I’ve been terribly careless, almost as though I’ve failed at something I should have protected.\n\nThat’s the part I have trouble letting go of. I can tell myself, “You’re careful, you wouldn’t just toss something important,” but the feeling doesn’t settle until I check. It’s as if I can’t quite allow myself to move on while there’s even the tiniest possibility that I misplaced something important.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you worry that something bad will happen to someone else if you don't check these things?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 12\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }

{"key":"S065","name":"Obsessions","description":"Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.","reason":"The doctor is asking about the intrusive worry or feared consequence that drives the checking, which is most directly an obsession."}

I might leave things looking quite messy, actually, because I’m still going through them or checking them. So it’s not that I feel comfortable only if the room is perfectly arranged. It’s more… I don’t know, I feel unsettled if I can’t trust that an important paper or keepsake is in the right folder or box.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really noticed a need for a specific pattern or arrangement for its own sake. It’s the fear that I’ve made some mistake with something that matters, even if I don’t have a real reason to think I have.\n\nIt’s not just, “Where did I put that?” It’s more like this little thought gets stuck in my mind—what if I threw away my husband’s old tax papers, or what if I put that family recipe in the wrong box—and even when I know better, even when I can tell myself I’m careful and I probably just saw it, it keeps needling at me. I can’t seem to just let it pass.\n\nAnd then it starts to feel as if I’ve done something terribly careless, almost irresponsible, and that’s the part that bothers me so much. I feel I have to check again just to quiet that feeling, even though another part of me knows it doesn’t really make sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong sense of responsibility to ensure these important papers and keepsakes are safe and in their proper place?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Very much so, I think. It feels like it’s my responsibility to keep track of those things, especially the papers or keepsakes that have some meaning or importance attached to them.\n\nEven when I know, in a sensible part of my mind, that I probably haven’t done anything wrong, the thought will keep needling at me. I’ll think, what if I threw away my husband’s old tax papers? Or what if I put that family recipe in the wrong box, and later no one can find it because of me? And then it doesn’t feel like just a small mistake. It starts to feel as if I’ve been terribly careless, almost as though I’ve failed at something I should have protected.\n\nThat’s the part I have trouble letting go of.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not exactly that something bad will happen to someone else, no… at least I don’t usually think of it in such a clear way. It’s more that I get this thought stuck in my mind that I may have done something careless with something important.

Like I’ll suddenly think, what if I threw away my husband’s old tax papers, or what if I put a family recipe in the wrong box and then no one can find it later? And even if I know, really, that I’m careful and I probably didn’t do that, the thought keeps needling at me. It’s very hard to just let it pass.

So it’s less like I picture a terrible accident happening to someone, and more like I feel I’ve done something terribly careless or irresponsible if I don’t check. And that feeling can be very hard to sit with.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came because I’m spending just far too much of my day checking things in the house. It’s mostly folders, drawers, and these labeled boxes I keep for important papers and little keepsakes—things I really don’t want misplaced. I’ll open a folder to make sure the documents are still there, then close it, and then a few minutes later I feel I have to open it again. Same with drawers and boxes. I know I just looked, I do know that, but it doesn’t seem to settle it for me.\n\nIt’s gotten to where I’ll go around checking the same places again and again, and it takes up a ridiculous amount of time. I feel embarrassed saying that at my age, but it’s become hard to just leave things alone once I’ve checked them.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and make you feel anxious or uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not just that I remember to check something, it’s more like a thought starts poking at me and won’t leave me alone. I’ll think, “What if I threw away my husband’s old tax papers?” even though I know perfectly well I wouldn’t just toss something like that without noticing. Or I’ll suddenly worry that I put one of the family recipes in the wrong box, the ones I try to keep separate because they mean something to me.\n\nAnd the thing is, I can tell myself, “You know better, you’re careful, you already looked,” but it doesn’t seem to matter much. The thought keeps needling at me, like I’ve done something terribly careless and just haven’t discovered it yet. It makes me feel very uneasy, almost guilty, until I go and make sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check these things in a specific way or order to feel okay?\n\nPatient: Not exactly in a neat order, no. It’s more that I feel I have to open the same folder or drawer or one of those labeled boxes and look inside until I’m convinced, at least for the moment, that the papers or keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nSometimes I’ll check a folder, close it, walk away, and then almost immediately I’m thinking, “Did I really see it?” So I go back and open it again. And then I might move to a drawer, then back to the same box, especially if it has something important in it. It isn’t like a ritual with counting or anything like that, but it has to be checked properly, if that makes sense—actually looking, not just glancing. Otherwise I don’t feel settled. Even when I only checked a few minutes before, I’ll still feel pulled to do it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have no choice but to check these things, like you're being forced to do it?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s a good way to put it, unfortunately. I don’t feel like I’m choosing it in the ordinary sense, like, “Oh, I’ll just tidy up and look through that drawer.” It feels more like I can’t leave it alone once the thought is there.\n\nI’ll have just opened a folder and seen the paper sitting right there, or checked one of my labeled boxes and seen the keepsake where it belongs, and I’ll close it and think, “All right, that’s done.” But then a few minutes later there’s this pressure to go back. It’s as if my mind won’t accept the answer I just gave it. I know I checked, but I still feel pushed to open it again and make sure.\n\nSo yes, I do feel forced by it, in a way. Not by anyone else, of course, but by this uncomfortable feeling that I won’t be settled unless I check the same folders, drawers, and boxes again. And it’s taking up far too much of my day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel the need to perform any specific physical actions, like washing your hands or tapping, in order to feel okay?\n\nPatient: Not washing my hands or tapping, no, not really like that. It’s more the opening and looking that I seem to get stuck doing.\n\nI came because I’m spending far too much of my day going back to the same folders, the same drawers, and those labeled boxes I keep for important papers and keepsakes. I’ll open one to make sure something is where I left it, and even if I see it there, even if I only checked a few minutes before, I feel this need to open it again and look properly. It’s a physical thing in the sense that I’m getting up, pulling the drawer out, lifting the lid, sorting through the folder, making sure the paper or item is actually there.\n\nSo no, it isn’t a particular action like tapping three times or washing, but I do feel I have to do that checking action over and over before I can feel okay, at least for a little while.\n\nDoctor: Is this checking behavior affecting your relationships or daily activities in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. More than I like to admit, honestly.\n\nI’m not working now, so I used to think, “Well, at least it’s only my own time I’m wasting,” but it isn’t just that. I’ve had to cancel mentoring sessions because I’ll start checking a folder or one of those boxes before I’m meant to leave or get on the phone, and then suddenly the whole afternoon has slipped away. By the time I look up, I’m upset with myself and it feels too late or I’m too flustered to go through with it properly.\n\nAnd I’ve skipped book club too, which bothers me because that’s one of the things I really look forward to. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to make sure one thing is put away, and then I get caught going back through the drawers and boxes again, and then I’m late, and then embarrassed, and then I just don’t go.\n\nIt affects having people over as well. My apartment gets left in disarray from all the checking—folders out, drawers open, things not put back neatly because I’m still “making sure,” if that makes sense. Then I feel too embarrassed to invite anyone in or have anyone stop by. So yes, it’s interfering with my day and with seeing people. It’s not destroying everything, but it’s significant enough that I feel quite ashamed of how much it’s narrowing my life.\n\nDoctor: Are you checking these things because you're afraid something bad will happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: Yes… though it isn’t usually that I think something dramatic will happen, like a fire or an accident or anything like that. It’s more that I get this awful thought that I’ve been terribly careless with something important.\n\nFor example, even when part of me knows better, I’ll think, “What if I threw away my husband’s old tax papers?” or, “What if I put that family recipe in the wrong box and now I won’t be able to find it when someone asks for it?” And I can tell myself, “No, you just saw it,” or “You wouldn’t have thrown that out,” but the thought keeps needling at me. It doesn’t feel satisfied with a reasonable answer.\n\nSo I suppose the bad thing I’m afraid of is that I’ll have lost or misplaced something that mattered, and that it would be my fault because I didn’t check carefully enough. It feels like I’ve done something terribly irresponsible, even when I know, rationally, that I probably haven’t. And that feeling is what sends me back to the box or the folder again.\n\nDoctor: Is the thought of something being lost or misplaced the only thing that drives you to check, or are there other fears or concerns behind it?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly that, yes—the thought that something important has been lost or put in the wrong place. But it has a kind of… moral feeling to it, if that makes sense. It isn’t just, “Oh dear, where is that paper?” It becomes, “How could I have been so careless?” or “What if I threw away my husband’s old tax papers and now they’re gone because of me?” Even when I know, really, that I’m careful with those things.\n\nOr with something like a family recipe—I’ll think, “What if I put it in the wrong box?” and then my mind won’t let it rest there. It starts feeling as though I’ve failed in some important responsibility, even if I have no real evidence that anything is missing. I can picture the folder, I can remember seeing it, and still this little needling thought keeps going: maybe you didn’t look properly, maybe it wasn’t the right paper, maybe you only assumed it was there.\n\nSo no, I don’t think it’s a lot of different fears, exactly. It’s that fear of having misplaced or thrown away something meaningful, and underneath that, the awful feeling that I’ve done something terribly careless and can’t just let it pass.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be in a specific order or arranged in a certain way for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, no. I mean, I do like things to be reasonably tidy, but I don’t think I have a particular order they must be in, like everything lined up just so, or arranged by size or color or anything like that.\n\nFor me it’s less about how things look and more about whether I can feel sure something is where it’s supposed to be. I might leave things looking quite messy, actually, because I’m still going through them or checking them. So it’s not that I feel comfortable only if the room is perfectly arranged. It’s more… I don’t know, I feel unsettled if I can’t trust that an important paper or keepsake is in the right folder or box.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really noticed a need for a specific pattern or arrangement for its own sake. It’s not like the chair has to be angled a certain way or the books have to be in a certain sequence.
